Each card is one question a research library held at the end of the week of 22 to 28 September 2026, with the answer it gave and what the answer rests on. Two cards show a matter with no question. For each card, mark the two lines at its foot; a few words help but are optional. Three cards carry a blue sheet with one or two more lines per fact. Open a passage, the complete request or an article whenever you want to: that is normal reading. Nothing here says which system wrote a card, and some cards are controls. About half an hour. Your marks are kept in this browser as you go; when you finish, copy the box at the very end and paste it back.

Card 2 of 19

Will Nvidia agree to invest in Anthropic's IPO as an anchor investor by September 26, 2027?

About NVDA (the company) · due 2027-09-26 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Yes: Nvidia publicly agrees to invest as an anchor investor in Anthropic's IPO by September 26, 2027. ← the present answer
  2. No: Nvidia has not publicly agreed to invest as an anchor investor in Anthropic's IPO by September 26, 2027.

Its reasons

The reference is rung 2: no public agreement is reported, and no separate market consensus is supplied. The reported talks for the specific anchor-investor role, with a possible investment of up to $10 billion, make a public agreement by the deadline more likely than not. A possible delay until after the November 2026 elections still leaves time before September 26, 2027, so the evidence supports rung 1 rather than merely describing an unconfirmed agreement today.

What it expects: A public agreement by September 26, 2027 is more likely than not. Reuters reports talks and a possible investment of up to $10 billion; even if the IPO slips past the November 2026 U.S. midterm elections, the reported timing leaves several months before the deadline.

Card 3 of 19

What total amount in USD of NVIDIA’s credit-support commitments and minimum-revenue guarantees or backstops to AI labs and cloud partners, including the support for the additional lab’s nearly 2 gigawatts of computing capacity, will be disclosed in NVIDIA’s FY2027 Form 10-K and related quantified disclosures by the annual report’s publication?

About NVDA (the company) · due 2027-04-01 · answer as of 2026-09-22

Answer choices

  1. Strongly bullish: below 10,000,000,000 USD ← the present answer
  2. Bullish: from 10,000,000,000 USD to below 25,000,000,000 USD
  3. Neutral: from 25,000,000,000 USD to below 50,000,000,000 USD (no reference figure when the question opened)
  4. Bearish: from 50,000,000,000 USD to below 100,000,000,000 USD
  5. Strongly bearish: 100,000,000,000 USD or more

Its reasons

The question opened with no quantified market or company reference for the total; the ladder labels rung 3 (Neutral) as having no reference figure. The evidence now points above the rung-5 threshold: NVIDIA is reported to have disclosed maximum guarantee exposure reaching $105 billion under defined conditions. The 2-GW support and other facility backstops reinforce the direction but are not separately quantified here, so I do not add them to the $105 billion or treat the broader $175 billion financing estimate as all guarantees. The implied disclosed exposure is therefore around $105 billion or more, which is at least $100 billion and supports rung 5.

What it expects: Around $105 billion or more in disclosed maximum guarantee exposure, based on the reported figure of up to $105 billion under defined conditions. This clears the rung-5 boundary of $100 billion; no separate amounts are supplied to add for the nearly 2-GW lab support or other backstops.

Card 4 of 19

Will China's Ministry of Industry and Information Technology (MIIT) formally approve purchases of Nvidia's RTX Pro 5500 by Alibaba, ByteDance, or other Chinese firms by January 31, 2027?

About chip-export-controls (a theme) · due 2027-01-31 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Yes: MIIT formally approves by January 31, 2027, a purchase of RTX Pro 5500 chips by Alibaba, ByteDance, or another Chinese firm. ← the present answer
  2. Ruled out: After September 27, 2026, MIIT formally rejects all RTX Pro 5500 purchase plans from Alibaba, ByteDance, and other Chinese firms, or formally prohibits those firms from purchasing the chips. This rung holds when that, or any other decision against the event of rung 1 as a whole, or a formal abandonment or withdrawal of it as a whole, was made after 2026-09-27 and stands on 2027-01-31, and only when rung 1 does not.
  3. Still open: Neither rung 1 nor rung 2 holds on 2027-01-31.

Its reasons

The available reference is no explicit consensus probability; the reported expectation that MIIT intends to approve some purchase plans, and that some firms were told approval is likely, places the reference at rung 1. I also forecast rung 1: those reports are specific to the firms and product in the question, and plans were collected months before the deadline. The contemplated ByteDance order and reported China supply plan indicate possible scale, not an approved quantity. Domestic-chip preferences, a proposed bundling rule, uncertainty over U.S. export treatment, and the H200 precedent temper the forecast; the late-December shipment target also leaves a relatively short window before the deadline. But none is evidence of a formal decision against all purchases, and the reported MIIT intent makes at least one approval by January 31 more likely than either an adverse formal decision or no decision.

What it expects: Most likely, MIIT formally approves at least one purchase by January 31, 2027; the evidence supplies no approval date or approved quantity. The reported roughly 1 million-unit ByteDance plan would equal about two quarters of the reported 500,000-unit-per-quarter China supply plan, but neither figure establishes how much MIIT would approve.

Card 5 of 19

At NVIDIA’s next annual report, what amount of financing commitments to customers and partners will NVIDIA report in its commitments note?

About NVDA (the company) · due 2027-02-28 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Strongly bullish: Reported commitments total less than $1 billion.
  2. Bullish: Reported commitments total at least $1 billion but less than $5 billion.
  3. Neutral: Reported commitments total at least $5 billion but less than $10 billion.
  4. Bearish: Reported commitments total at least $10 billion but less than $25 billion.
  5. Strongly bearish: Reported commitments total at least $25 billion. ← the present answer

Its reasons

There is no supplied market consensus, company guidance, stated commitments-note target, or prior reported note total, so the reference cannot be placed on the ladder. The strongest direct scale indicators remain maximum guarantee-exposure disclosures of $105 billion and $108.5 billion; they are ceilings, not amounts to add together or assume will be fully reported. New evidence adds a signed $1 billion Nscale financing commitment and a disclosed guarantee of up to $860.3 million, while the reported insurance discussions could eventually shift risk but are early-stage and have produced no stated deal or amount. Taken together with the existing guarantee scale, the evidence still makes a reported amount of at least $25 billion more likely than a lower rung; it does not establish that the full guarantee caps will appear as the reported total.

What it expects: At least $25 billion reported, making rung 5 most likely; the $105 billion and $108.5 billion maximum guarantee disclosures establish the scale but are ceilings, not the implied reportable total. The $1 billion Nscale financing and $860.3 million guarantee add specific support, while no amount is supplied for the larger backstop offer or the risk-transfer discussions.

Card 6 of 19

Will NVIDIA complete (close) its agreed acquisition of Hugging Face, as disclosed in NVIDIA's filings, by March 24, 2028?

About NVDA (the company) · due 2028-03-24 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Yes: NVIDIA completes the Hugging Face acquisition by March 24, 2028, as evidenced by NVIDIA's filings or a completion announcement. ← the present answer
  2. Ruled out: After 2026-09-25, NVIDIA or Hugging Face formally terminates or withdraws the acquisition agreement before it is completed. This rung holds when that, or any other decision against the event of rung 1 as a whole, or a formal abandonment or withdrawal of it as a whole, was made after 2026-09-25 and stands on 2028-03-24, and only when rung 1 does not.
  3. Still open: Neither rung 1 nor rung 2 holds on 2028-03-24.

Its reasons

The agreement and the newer reports describing Hugging Face as acquired make completion by the deadline the most likely outcome. However, the reports describing it as completed do not provide the NVIDIA filing or explicit completion announcement specified by rung 1, and the contemporaneous “pending” report is contrary evidence. That limits certainty but does not make an open or terminated deal more likely by the 2028 check date.

What it expects: The evidence implies the deal is likely to have closed already or to close well before March 24, 2028: multiple reports describe Hugging Face as acquired, while the agreement was announced roughly 18 months before the check date. No precise closing date can be derived from the evidence.

Card 7 of 19

Will China's Ministry of Industry and Information Technology permit Alibaba and ByteDance to buy Nvidia RTX PRO 5500 cards by January 31, 2027?

About chip-export-controls (a theme) · due 2027-01-31 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Yes: MIIT permits Alibaba and ByteDance to buy RTX PRO 5500 cards by January 31, 2027. ← the present answer
  2. No: MIIT has not permitted Alibaba and ByteDance to buy RTX PRO 5500 cards by January 31, 2027.

Its reasons

The reference is rung 2 (no permission by the deadline): no market consensus or confirmed permission is supplied, so the reference is the no-permission status. The forecast is rung 1 because the report that MIIT intends to approve purchases, together with its request for Alibaba's and ByteDance's planned quantities and uses, makes permission by January 31 more likely than not. Nvidia's reported late-December shipment aim is consistent with that timing, though none of these reports establishes that permission has already been granted. Repeated coverage of the same underlying reports adds no independent confirmation.

What it expects: Most likely, MIIT permits both companies to buy the cards by January 31, 2027. The reports give no exact approval date or purchase volume; the reported intention to approve and late-December shipment aim support approval within the deadline.

Card 8 of 19

How much in purchase obligations and supply commitments will NVIDIA disclose in its FY2027 Form 10-K, as reported in the purchase obligations and contractual commitments disclosures and related notes?

About NVDA (the company) · due 2027-04-01 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Strongly bullish: below 50,000,000,000 USD
  2. Bullish: from 50,000,000,000 USD to below 100,000,000,000 USD
  3. Neutral: from 100,000,000,000 USD to below 150,000,000,000 USD (no reference figure when the question opened)
  4. Bearish: from 150,000,000,000 USD to below 200,000,000,000 USD
  5. Strongly bearish: 200,000,000,000 USD or more ← the present answer

Its reasons

There is no market consensus or company guidance for the FY2027 Form 10-K commitment total. The question's no-reference starting point is $100bn to below $150bn, rung 3. The latest quantified anchor is $279bn at FY2027 Q2, already above rung 5's $200bn boundary; the increase from $119bn in Q1, management's expectation that supply remains constrained through FY2028, and sustained growth guidance make a year-end disclosure at or above $200bn more likely than a fall below it. The evidence does not support adding the separately reported $500bn HBM figure: its period and overlap with the $279bn commitment total are unspecified. The newer TSMC-order report is a rumor involving several customers, so it reinforces direction but does not establish an additional Nvidia amount.

What it expects: Approximately $279bn or higher at the FY2027 10-K: $279bn was reported at FY2027 Q2, up from $119bn in Q1, and the supply-constraint and demand evidence supports commitments remaining elevated through year-end. This clears the $200bn rung-5 threshold; the separate $500bn HBM claim is not added because its timing and overlap are unspecified.

Card 9 of 19

By January 31, 2027, will U.S. authorities authorize Nvidia to sell Blackwell-generation AI chips to China and will Chinese authorities permit those shipments?

About chip-export-controls (a theme) · due 2027-01-31 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Yes: By January 31, 2027, U.S. authorities have authorized sales of Nvidia Blackwell-generation AI chips to China and Chinese authorities permit those shipments.
  2. Ruled out: After September 23, 2026, U.S. export-control authorities formally prohibit Blackwell exports to China, or Chinese authorities formally prohibit Nvidia Blackwell shipments, with the prohibition remaining in force through January 31, 2027. This rung holds when that, or any other decision against the event of rung 1 as a whole, or a formal abandonment or withdrawal of it as a whole, was made after 2026-09-23 and stands on 2027-01-31, and only when rung 1 does not.
  3. Still open: Neither rung 1 nor rung 2 holds on 2027-01-31. ← the present answer

Its reasons

The reference is NVIDIA’s outlook: it excludes China data-center compute revenue, which is closest to rung 3, Still open; it is not a forecast of a formal prohibition. The evidence supports some route to Chinese purchases of the Blackwell-generation RTX PRO 5500, but the reported Chinese intent is unconfirmed and unsized, and that product is a workstation GPU outside the main data-center accelerator category. Meanwhile, reporting says current-generation/high-end accelerators remain barred by the U.S. and China has restricted even H200 shipments. Those facts make joint approval and permission by the due date less likely than continued openness without the required decision, but do not establish a qualifying post-September 23 formal prohibition or abandonment that remains in force. The new quantified H200 order, inventory, licence and Chinese-use restrictions strengthen the case against broad shipment resumption; they do not make the RTX PRO report a confirmed shipment or a data-center approval.

What it expects: By January 31, 2027, the most likely outcome is still no established joint U.S. authorization and Chinese permission for Blackwell data-center AI accelerators; the only specific Blackwell purchase path reported is the unsized, unconfirmed RTX PRO 5500 workstation proposal. That points to rung 3: the evidence does not show a qualifying post-September 23 formal prohibition or abandonment that remains in force through the due date.

Answer choices

  1. below 700,000,000,000 USD
  2. from 700,000,000,000 USD to below 775,000,000,000 USD
  3. from 775,000,000,000 USD to below 825,000,000,000 USD: from 775,000,000,000 USD to below 825,000,000,000 USD (the reference when the question opened: "the big five AI hyperscalers will spend nearly $800 billion on data centers this year") ← the present answer
  4. from 825,000,000,000 USD to below 900,000,000,000 USD
  5. 900,000,000,000 USD or more

Its reasons

The reference is nearly $800 billion, which sits in rung 3. The direct forecast still implies about $800 billion for the five in calendar 2026, within rung 3's $775 billion–below-$825 billion range. Alphabet's $200 billion is a component of the aggregate, not an additional amount; the GPU and capacity commitments and supplier/customer demand indicators lean upward but do not quantify a 2026 total or revision that crosses a rung boundary. The global AI-infrastructure figure near $750 billion has a different scope, and the $1.3 trillion projections concern 2027.

What it expects: About $800 billion, based on NVIDIA's forecast for the big five's 2026 data-center capex as reported in e162 and e367, consistent with the near-$800-billion estimate in e103 and e119. This is within rung 3; Alphabet's $200 billion is part of the total, not an amount to add to it.

Card 11 of 19

At NVIDIA’s next annual report, what amount of financing commitments to customers and partners will NVIDIA report in its commitments note?

About NVDA (the company) · due 2027-02-28 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Strongly bullish: Reported commitments total less than $1 billion.
  2. Bullish: Reported commitments total at least $1 billion but less than $5 billion.
  3. Neutral: Reported commitments total at least $5 billion but less than $10 billion.
  4. Bearish: Reported commitments total at least $10 billion but less than $25 billion.
  5. Strongly bearish: Reported commitments total at least $25 billion. ← the present answer

Its reasons

There is no supplied market consensus, company guidance, stated commitments-note target, or prior reported note total, so the reference cannot be placed on the ladder. The strongest direct scale indicators remain maximum guarantee-exposure disclosures of $105 billion and $108.5 billion; they are ceilings, not amounts to add together or assume will be fully reported. New evidence adds a signed $1 billion Nscale financing commitment and a disclosed guarantee of up to $860.3 million, while the reported insurance discussions could eventually shift risk but are early-stage and have produced no stated deal or amount. Taken together with the existing guarantee scale, the evidence still makes a reported amount of at least $25 billion more likely than a lower rung; it does not establish that the full guarantee caps will appear as the reported total.

What it expects: At least $25 billion reported, making rung 5 most likely; the $105 billion and $108.5 billion maximum guarantee disclosures establish the scale but are ceilings, not the implied reportable total. The $1 billion Nscale financing and $860.3 million guarantee add specific support, while no amount is supplied for the larger backstop offer or the risk-transfer discussions.

Card 12 of 19

Will NVIDIA disclose in its quarterly results or FY2027 Form 10-K that Vera Rubin made volume customer shipments with first revenue recognition by the end of FY2027 (January 31, 2027)?

About NVDA (the company) · due 2027-01-31 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Yes: NVIDIA's quarterly results or FY2027 Form 10-K show that Vera Rubin made volume customer shipments with first revenue recognition by January 31, 2027. ← the present answer
  2. Ruled out: After September 23, 2026, NVIDIA formally cancels or abandons the Vera Rubin platform before it makes volume customer shipments. This rung holds when that, or any other decision against the event of rung 1 as a whole, or a formal abandonment or withdrawal of it as a whole, was made after 2026-09-23 and stands on 2027-01-31, and only when rung 1 does not.
  3. Still open: Neither rung 1 nor rung 2 holds on 2027-01-31.

Its reasons

There is no explicit consensus for this exact event. The closest company reference is management's expectation that Vera Rubin contributes about 20% of fiscal Q3 2027 Data Center revenue, which sits at rung 1 because, if realized, it implies recognized revenue from material customer shipments before the deadline. The August production-shipment reports and management's expectation that sales to the largest cloud companies accelerate in Q4 as supply grows reinforce that forecast. Super Micro's reported commercial shipments add shipment support, although they do not establish NVIDIA's own reported revenue recognition. Supply constraints and the analyst view that some revenue may be delayed temper the outlook, but do not outweigh the specific Q3 revenue expectation and Q4 acceleration. No evidence indicates a qualifying cancellation or abandonment.

What it expects: The evidence implies Vera Rubin generates about 20% of fiscal Q3 2027 Data Center revenue, with sales to the largest cloud customers expected to accelerate again in fiscal Q4 as supply grows. This supports volume customer shipments and first revenue recognition by January 31, 2027, though it remains a forecast rather than an established result.

Card 13 of 19

Will NVIDIA complete (close) its agreed acquisition of Hugging Face, as disclosed in NVIDIA's filings, by March 24, 2028?

About NVDA (the company) · due 2028-03-24 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Yes: NVIDIA completes the Hugging Face acquisition by March 24, 2028, as evidenced by NVIDIA's filings or a completion announcement. ← the present answer
  2. Ruled out: After 2026-09-25, NVIDIA or Hugging Face formally terminates or withdraws the acquisition agreement before it is completed. This rung holds when that, or any other decision against the event of rung 1 as a whole, or a formal abandonment or withdrawal of it as a whole, was made after 2026-09-25 and stands on 2028-03-24, and only when rung 1 does not.
  3. Still open: Neither rung 1 nor rung 2 holds on 2028-03-24.

Its reasons

The agreement and the newer reports describing Hugging Face as acquired make completion by the deadline the most likely outcome. However, the reports describing it as completed do not provide the NVIDIA filing or explicit completion announcement specified by rung 1, and the contemporaneous “pending” report is contrary evidence. That limits certainty but does not make an open or terminated deal more likely by the 2028 check date.

What it expects: The evidence implies the deal is likely to have closed already or to close well before March 24, 2028: multiple reports describe Hugging Face as acquired, while the agreement was announced roughly 18 months before the check date. No precise closing date can be derived from the evidence.

Card 15 of 19

What gross margin will NVIDIA report for FY2028?

About NVDA (the company) · due 2028-02-28 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Strongly bullish: FY2028 gross margin is at least 75%.
  2. Bullish: FY2028 gross margin is at least 73% but below 75%.
  3. Neutral: FY2028 gross margin is at least 71% but below 73%. ← the present answer
  4. Bearish: FY2028 gross margin is at least 69% but below 71%.
  5. Strongly bearish: FY2028 gross margin is below 69%.

Its reasons

The reference is NVIDIA management’s FY2028 gross-margin guidance of 72%–73%, which sits in rung 3; Trefis also reports consensus expects roughly steady margins. The implied FY2028 outcome remains about 72.5% (the midpoint of guidance): Q4 FY2027 is expected to bottom at 71%–72%, and the FY2028 outlook includes price increases taking effect in Q1. The more detailed Q3 guide and memory-cost pressure reinforce near-term downside, but no evidence quantifies a further FY2028 reduction below the company’s 72%–73% range. Thus the forecast remains above 71% and below 73%, without crossing a rung boundary.

What it expects: About 72.5% for FY2028, using the midpoint of management’s 72%–73% guidance. Management expects a 71%–72% FY2027 Q4 bottom and says FY2028 margins settle at 72%–73%, including the effect of price increases taking effect in FY2028 Q1.

How much will the five largest AI hyperscalers spend in total on data centers in calendar 2026, as shown by their Q4/full-year 2026 earnings and capex disclosures?

About ai-capex (a theme) · due 2027-03-31 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. below 700,000,000,000 USD
  2. from 700,000,000,000 USD to below 775,000,000,000 USD
  3. from 775,000,000,000 USD to below 825,000,000,000 USD: from 775,000,000,000 USD to below 825,000,000,000 USD (the reference when the question opened: "the big five AI hyperscalers will spend nearly $800 billion on data centers this year") ← the present answer
  4. from 825,000,000,000 USD to below 900,000,000,000 USD
  5. 900,000,000,000 USD or more

Its reasons

The reference is nearly $800 billion, which sits in rung 3. The direct forecast still implies about $800 billion for the five in calendar 2026, within rung 3's $775 billion–below-$825 billion range. Alphabet's $200 billion is a component of the aggregate, not an additional amount; the GPU and capacity commitments and supplier/customer demand indicators lean upward but do not quantify a 2026 total or revision that crosses a rung boundary. The global AI-infrastructure figure near $750 billion has a different scope, and the $1.3 trillion projections concern 2027.

What it expects: About $800 billion, based on NVIDIA's forecast for the big five's 2026 data-center capex as reported in e162 and e367, consistent with the near-$800-billion estimate in e103 and e119. This is within rung 3; Alphabet's $200 billion is part of the total, not an amount to add to it.

The implied total therefore remains within rung 3's $775 billion–below-$825 billion interval.

Card 18 of 19

How much will Nvidia's total revenue grow in FY2028?

About NVDA (the company) · due 2028-02-29 · answer as of 2026-09-28

Answer choices

  1. Strongly bullish: FY2028 total revenue growth is at least 80%.
  2. Bullish: FY2028 total revenue growth is at least 70% and below 80%. ← the present answer
  3. Neutral: FY2028 total revenue growth is at least 45% and below 70%.
  4. Bearish: FY2028 total revenue growth is at least 25% and below 45%.
  5. Strongly bearish: FY2028 total revenue growth is below 25%.

Its reasons

The market reference is roughly 65.5%–66% FY2028 growth, which is rung 3 (45% to below 70%). NVIDIA's approximately 70% FY2028 guidance is the stronger forecast input and sits exactly at rung 2's lower boundary. The supply bottleneck expected to last through FY2028 constrains the upside implied by customer demand; the AWS and federal GPU deployments support demand but span multiple years and do not provide enough FY2028 revenue or pricing detail to raise the implied outcome above the guide. Thus the implied outcome remains about 70%, rung 2.

What it expects: About 70% FY2028 growth: management's direct outlook is approximately 70%, and applying that growth to the cited $412 billion FY2027 consensus base implies roughly $700 billion of FY2028 revenue (e199). The supply constraint expected through FY2028 limits the case for exceeding that outlook.

  4. Bearish: FY2027 data-center segment revenue is at least $300 billion but below $350 billion.
  5. Strongly bearish: FY2027 data-center segment revenue is below $300 billion.

Its reasons

The reference cannot be placed: no consensus, company guidance, or stated FY2027 target is supplied. The latest quarter's $89 billion of data-center revenue is a strong scale indicator and points toward a high annual total, but it does not establish the full-year run-rate or its growth trajectory. I therefore forecast the central $350–<$400 billion band rather than treating a flat annualization of the latest quarter as an FY2027 estimate.

What it expects: A precise FY2027 figure cannot be derived from the supplied evidence. The latest quarter was $89 billion of data-center revenue; simply multiplying that quarter by four would imply $356 billion, but flat annualization is not a valid projection here, so the forecast is the $350–<$400 billion band without a defensible point estimate.

Nvidia stock is approaching its cheapest valuation in a decade despite booming profits

qz.com · 2026-09-22

the stored text (3,176 characters)
Nvidia stock is trading at less than 17 times forward earnings, half the multiple it commanded in 2025, even as revenue and net income are expected to surge
Nvidia $NVDA stock is trading near its lowest valuation in more than a decade, according to Bloomberg , even as the chipmaker's revenue and net income are each expected to grow by roughly 90% or more in its current fiscal year.
At under 17 times forward earnings, the stock's multiple has been cut in half compared with 2025 — a period when Nvidia 's expansion was actually slower — and has fallen sharply from above 25 times expected profits just this past May, according to Bloomberg.
"The stock has de-rated pretty significantly, which suggests a healthy dose of skepticism that the company's current earnings power is sustainable," Eli Horton, a TCW senior portfolio manager overseeing thematic and durable growth equities, told Bloomberg. "The stock's performance is surprising, given the backdrop of incredible fundamentals, but it tells you the market is expecting less than what the consensus is currently estimating."
One drag on the stock's valuation is a squeeze on profitability. Nvidia 's gross margin came in at 75% last quarter but analyst estimates compiled by Bloomberg show it sliding under 72% by the fourth quarter, with a recovery expected thereafter. Rising costs for key components such as memory chips are a central factor.
Competition is another concern. David Russell, global head of market strategy at TradeStation, pointed to the growing threat of customers building their own silicon, noting that Meta $META Platforms and Alphabet $GOOGL are among the major Nvidia buyers that have moved to design AI chips internally — a shift he believes will gradually erode Nvidia 's pricing power and market share. "Multiples expand when companies are well positioned with potential to get better, and Nvidia doesn't offer that," Russell said.
Nvidia stock has gained 22% so far in 2026, putting it second among the Magnificent Seven behind Apple $AAPL 's 25% advance — yet that performance looks modest against the broader chip sector. The Philadelphia Stock Exchange Semiconductor Index has surged nearly 76%, with Intel $INTC and Advanced Micro Devices both more than tripling; Nvidia sits near the bottom of the index's leaderboard, even as the index itself carries a valuation of 20 times forward earnings.
Nvidia Chief Executive Officer Jensen Huang has pushed back on the market's assessment, calling the company "the world's first and only growth value stock" and describing Nvidia as "incredibly misunderstood" at a Goldman Sachs $GS technology conference earlier this month.
The valuation discount follows a stretch of strong results. Nvidia stock climbed roughly 7% after its second-quarter earnings , when revenue more than doubled and the company projected 70% revenue growth in fiscal 2028 — well above the 45% analysts had expected at the time.
Horton said the current multiple looks favorable given that neither a pullback in AI infrastructure spending nor a major regulatory intervention appears imminent. "This seems like a very favorable multiple to have as an entry point," he said.

Nvidia's Demand Is Outpacing Its 70% Growth Forecast, and That Is Just the Beginning

fool.com · 2026-09-23

the stored text (3,392 characters)
There may be no better company to represent the AI boom than Nvidia (NASDAQ: NVDA). Its graphics processing units (GPUs) are the backbone of artificial intelligence data centers, and in just three years, the company's market cap has skyrocketed by 445% to $5.5 trillion, making it the most valuable company in the world .
What continues to impress many investors is that Nvidia isn't done growing yet. In its fiscal 2027 second-quarter report, Management forecast that in fiscal 2028, its sales will rise by 70%, thanks to continued demand for AI chips.
But what's really interesting is that Nvidia leadership said it could double its sales in fiscal 2028 if it were able to overcome the constraints on the supply of commodities needed to produce its hardware.
In short, demand for Nvidia's chips is far outpacing the company's own forecast sales grown. Here's what's happening and why it's good news for Nvidia investors.
On Nvidia's second-quarter fiscal 2027 earnings call , Nvidia CFO Colette Kress said:
Incredibly, we are seeing demand acceleration even at our scale. Customer forecasts point to our growth doubling next year. However, as I mentioned earlier, we expect to grow approximately 70% as we are supply-constrained.
Kress is absolutely right. This level of sales growth is incredible for a $5.5 trillion company. To put it in perspective, Apple 's market cap is just under $5 trillion, and its revenue growth was 16% in the third quarter to $109 billion. Still impressive for its size, but nowhere near Nvidia's growth.
There are a handful of reasons why demand for artificial intelligence processors remains high, the first of which is that agentic AI is replacing human-generated prompts as the main driver of workload growth. In short, AI is doing more tasks on its own, and that's driving computing needs much higher.
Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said on the earnings call that "the amount of compute necessary is just extraordinary" because AI agents use between 15 and 100 times as much compute as would be required with a human guiding the work. All of this is combining to boost demand for Nvidia's GPUs.
Huang's not the only one projecting far more spending on AI infrastructure. S&P Global recently forecast that tech companies will spend an estimated $1.3 trillion on AI next year.
Sometimes it can be a problem when a company can't meet its customers' demand. But in this case, it's not so much of a problem because the entire tech industry -- including nearly all hardware makers -- is struggling to keep up.
For example, Micron Technology 's memory chips are having a significant problem, with demand far outpacing supply. That's led to skyrocketing memory prices, higher margins for Micron, and global supply constraints.
The big picture for Nvidia investors is that agentic AI could drive GPU demand much higher than it is now and will likely continue to boost Nvidia's sales for years to come. As Huang noted on the call:
Today, the vast majority of AI is prompted by people. I believe that this last month, it has crossed. Most AI are now agentic. But in the future, every company will have a whole bunch of agents.
Nvidia says it's working to increase supply to meet customer demand. If it succeeds, it could double its sales in fiscal 2028. But even if it doesn't, Nvidia appears to be on the cusp of another surge in processor demand that could last for years.

Nvidia Stock Set For 40% Surge: 3 Catalysts That Could Drive the Next Big Move

finance.yahoo.com · 2026-09-23

the stored text (3,325 characters)
NVDA earns a BUY with a $310.20 target (~40% upside), driven by the Vera Rubin ramp and $500 billion in mobilized AI infrastructure capital.
AMD's trailing P/E of 211 dwarfs NVDA's 45, while AVGO's AI semiconductor revenue surged 221%, confirming the AI infrastructure wave underpinning NVDA's outlook.
My 24/7 Wall St. price target for NVIDIA ( NASDAQ:NVDA ) is $310.20, which implies 39.56% upside from the current $225.35 quote. I rate NVDA a buy with high conviction.
Three catalysts drive the call: the Vera Rubin production ramp, more than $500 billion in third-party AI infrastructure capital being mobilized through NVIDIA partners, and the opening chapter of physical AI across robotics and autonomous vehicles.
NVDA is up 21.11% year to date and 28.17% over the trailing year. Q2 fiscal 2027 delivered revenue of $96.22 billion, up 105.85% year over year, with non-GAAP EPS of $2.22 beating consensus by 6.29%. Data Center revenue hit $89.023 billion. Management guided Q3 to $108 billion.
Why Bulls See $353 or Higher
The bull-case one-year target is $353.54. Jensen Huang told investors NVIDIA can supply only 70% of demand, with fiscal 2028 growth pegged at approximately 70%. Vera Rubin ramps platform value per gigawatt from Blackwell's $25 billion to $40 billion.
Hyperscaler capex is expected to reach nearly 800 billion in 2026 and 1.3 trillion in 2027, a wave that also pulls in the power, cooling, and networking suppliers we profiled in a free report on seven AI infrastructure names that aren't chipmakers. Analyst sentiment shows 57 Buy or Strong Buy ratings against 2 Holds and 1 Sell.
The bear scenario bottoms at $261.39, still above current price. Real risks include supply commitments of $279 billion, guarantee obligations capped at $108.5 billion, and DSO extending to 60 days.
Q3 guidance excludes China Data Center compute revenue citing geopolitical uncertainty. Gross margins are expected to bottom in the 71% to 72% range in Q4 on memory cost inflation, recovering to 72% to 73% in fiscal 2028 as volumes scale.
Advanced Micro Devices ( NASDAQ:AMD ) posted Q2 fiscal 2026 revenue of $11.536 billion, up 50.11%, with Data Center up 107%. AMD trades at a trailing P/E of 211 versus NVDA's 45, making NVDA the cheaper name on growth per valuation dollar.
Broadcom ( NASDAQ:AVGO ) competes in custom AI ASICs and networking. Q3 fiscal 2026 revenue reached $29.591 billion, up 85.5%, with AI semiconductor revenue of $16.70 billion growing 221% year over year. Its acceleration validates NVDA's guided $108.0 billion quarter sits inside a real industry wave. The peer set makes $310.20 look reasonable.
I'd own NVDA at $225.35 with a 24/7 Wall St. price target of $310.20 and a buy rating at 90% confidence. Supply-constrained demand into a Vera Rubin ramp management calls the fastest in company history drives the call.
I'd stay a buyer if hyperscaler capex tracks toward 1.3 trillion in 2027. I'd step aside if memory pricing pushes gross margin below 71% or if China restrictions widen beyond the Data Center compute carve-out.
These projections assume NVIDIA executes on the Vera Rubin ramp and hyperscaler AI spending holds. Significant upside or downside could result from broader China market access, a major AI capex reset, or accelerated adoption of physical AI and robotics platforms.

Nvidia Director Is Cashing Out $300M. Should You Be Worried?

finance.yahoo.com · 2026-09-23

the stored text (4,716 characters)
NVDA director Mark Stevens sold roughly $300M in shares outside a prearranged plan, yet the stock still gained 23% year to date.
Multiple NVIDIA insiders including Jensen Huang and CFO Colette Kress also sold shares in September, suggesting broad scheduled liquidity rather than a bearish signal.
Q2 fiscal 2027 revenue surged 106% year over year to $96B, making the director's trim a portfolio move, not a business warning.
An NVIDIA ( NASDAQ:NVDA ) director sold a block of stock worth roughly $300 million in early September, and the awkward part is that the stock didn't seem to care. NVIDIA closed at $228.87 on September 22, 2026, and shares are up 7.87% over the past week and 23.01% year to date. This is the latest in a run of insider disposals.
An insider sale carries minimal information and substantial noise. The discipline is separating what a Form 4 filing actually discloses from what a bearish market wants it to mean, especially at a $5.53 trillion company whose chief executive just told you demand runs well above supply.
What the Filing Discloses, and What It Does Not
Director Mark A. Stevens recorded disposals through a family trust between August 31 and September 4, 2026, with individual trades priced roughly between $220 and $234. Verified blocks include 447,400 shares at $220.0959 and four separate 300,000-share trades on September 2.
The filing was not reported under a prearranged trading plan. A prearranged plan lets an insider schedule sales in advance, removing discretion and timing questions.
Form 4 filings disclose no motive. The weighted average prices reflect a block worked across the market, which is how a stake this size gets moved without dislocating the stock.
Family trust sales routinely serve estate planning, diversification, and tax timing. A director who has held since the early days sits on a position no sensible risk manager would leave concentrated.
The insider dataset does not provide the remaining ownership stake, so the residual is unknown but almost certainly still very large. That matters because a director who has held since the early days would retain a substantial position even after a sale of this size.
The stock has run hard. Trimming into a 7.87% weekly move with a 23.01% year-to-date gain is what a long-term holder with an enormous embedded gain does.
A large sale outside a prearranged plan reads as a sentiment signal. It landed as Bloomberg wrote that NVIDIA's stock is flashing a warning sign as valuation falls, and Reddit chatter softened, with a bearish sentiment score of 38 on the stocks subreddit at midday September 22.
The louder concern is circular financing. NVIDIA has invested nearly $50 billion in the Frontier AI Labs and helped arrange over $500 billion of third-party capital with six infrastructure providers. CEO Jensen Huang addressed the skepticism directly: "We recognize the scale of this support, and we know some will call this circular financing. We see it differently."
Sales carry limited signal because there are many reasons to sell and essentially one reason to buy, which is why buys are more informative.
Other insiders disposed in the same window. Jensen Huang, CFO Colette Kress, EVP Debora Shoquist and general counsel Timothy Teter all recorded disposals on September 16 or 17, and director Tench Coxe recorded 500,000-share dispositions on July 1, August 5 and September 2. This looks like scheduled liquidity across the board.
Watch two things: whether a further notice of proposed sale appears signaling more to come, and whether any insider buying shows up on the other side. Either signal would carry more information than the disposals themselves.
Bull: the business is compounding. Q2 fiscal 2027 revenue was $96.22 billion, up 105.8% year over year, with a Q3 guide of $108 billion at the midpoint and a preliminary fiscal 2028 growth expectation of approximately 70%. An insider trimming a decades-old position tells you about his portfolio rather than about next quarter's orders.
Bear: the sale is large, outside a prearranged plan, and arrives as the market questions whether the spending cycle is self-funded. Supply commitments surged to $279 billion, and guarantee obligations are capped at $108.5 billion. Those are real balance-sheet exposures if demand slips.
Customer demand evidence decides the outcome. Track hyperscaler capital-spending guidance and the Vera Rubin ramp. Those tell you whether the compounding continues. The Form 4 tells you a wealthy man got liquid. If you would rather look past the chipmaker itself, the power, cooling, and networking suppliers behind the same buildout are worth knowing, and we profiled seven of them in a free AI infrastructure report.

NVIDIA's $279B Supply Commitments: Can These Secure Its Growth Runway?

the stored text (4,168 characters)
NVIDIA Corporation 's NVDA increase in supply and capacity commitments signals confidence in sustained artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure demand. At the end of the second quarter of fiscal 2027, the company had committed $279 billion to secure supply and production capacity, up from $119 billion in the first quarter. Most commitments are tied to memory and manufacturing facilities needed for current and future data center products. This gives NVIDIA greater visibility into component availability as it ramps up Blackwell and the next-generation Vera Rubin platform.
The timing is important. NVIDIA generated $96.22 billion in second-quarter fiscal 2027 revenues, up 106% year over year, while Data Center revenues surged 117% to $89.02 billion. The company expects third-quarter revenues of $108 billion (+/- 2%), indicating 89.5% year-over-year growth and a 12.2% sequential increase. Vera Rubin began production shipments in the third quarter.
The supply commitments could help NVIDIA avoid shortages that might otherwise limit revenue growth. Securing memory and critical components in advance should support smoother production as AI customers expand data center deployments. NVIDIA also expects fiscal 2028 revenue growth of about 70%, although supply constraints could limit how quickly it meets demand.
However, the commitments create risks. NVIDIA could face higher costs or excess inventory if customer demand slows or product transitions occur faster than expected. The company warns that changing demand could make commitments difficult to reduce.
Overall, the $279 billion commitment provides NVIDIA with a stronger supply base for its AI growth plans, but execution and demand will determine how effectively that capacity converts into revenues. The Zacks Consensus Estimate for fiscal 2027 revenues is currently pegged at $406.05 billion, indicating an 88% year-over-year increase.
AMD and Intel Are Challenging NVIDIA in AI Race
Advanced Micro Devices, Inc. AMD and Intel Corporation INTC are expanding their AI businesses.
Advanced Micro Devices is broadening its data center AI opportunity from Instinct accelerators to the Helios rack-scale platform. In the second quarter of 2026, AMD's Data Center revenues rose 107% year over year to $6.7 billion, while Instinct sales more than doubled. Helios is in production, with initial shipments expected late in the third quarter and a larger ramp-up in the fourth quarter and 2027.
The Helios platform has already garnered multiple large deals from big tech companies. Anthropic plans to deploy up to 2 gigawatts of MI450-series GPUs using the AMD Helios rack-scale platform, with the first gigawatt beginning in the first half of 2027. Microsoft plans to deploy Helios at scale on Azure. Advanced Micro Devices expects Data Center segment revenues to more than double year over year in 2027.
Intel is also gaining momentum in data center AI. Its second-quarter revenues rose 25% to $16.13 billion, while Data Center and AI revenues surged 59% to $6.26 billion. Intel also launched Xeon 6+ and expanded its open-source OpenVINO Physical AI framework, giving developers tools for AI and robotics applications.
Intel is building a broader AI infrastructure platform spanning CPUs, ASICs, graphics, networking and advanced packaging as customers design workload-specific systems. In the second quarter of 2026, purpose-built silicon revenues increased about 20% sequentially and nearly tripled year over year. During the last earnings call, management noted that purpose-built silicon revenues are approaching an estimated $2 billion run rate, and the company is targeting $4 billion in the near future.
Shares of NVIDIA have risen around 20.8% year to date, underperforming the Zacks Computer and Technology sector's gain of 23.4%.
From a valuation standpoint, NVDA trades at a forward price-to-earnings ratio of 17.11, below the sector's average of 21.45.
The Zacks Consensus Estimate for NVIDIA's fiscal 2027 and 2028 earnings implies a year-over-year increase of approximately 93.9% and 65.8%, respectively. Estimates for fiscal 2027 and 2028 have been revised upward over the past 30 days.

NVIDIA Stock Looks Expensive Until You Price The Vera Rubin Ramp

the stored text (3,233 characters)
NVIDIA Stock Looks Expensive Until You Price The Vera Rubin Ramp
NVIDIA (NVDA) stock trades near $225, about 28.3 times its adjusted earnings over the past twelve months. Adjusted here means normalized net income with stock-based compensation added back, the same broad basis analysts use in their forecasts. For a stock near its 52-week high, that looks steep. But the multiple leaves out the growth analysts already expect, and that growth depends on how much NVIDIA can supply.
NVIDIA Looks Ordinary Once The Forecast Earnings Arrive
On the earnings analysts expect for fiscal 2027, the same price is about 24.1 times. On what they expect for fiscal 2028, it is about 14.5 times. The price is the same. The earnings analysts expect it to buy are not.
NVIDIA expects Vera Rubin, its newest platform, to be the fastest product ramp in the company’s history. Management puts NVIDIA’s revenue opportunity at about $40 billion per gigawatt of capacity with Vera Rubin, against $25 billion with Blackwell. Production shipments began in August, with purchase orders already in from every major hyperscaler, AI cloud, and system OEM.
So What Stands Between NVIDIA And Those Earnings?
For fiscal 2028, management has guided about 70% revenue growth, the first time it has guided a full year ahead. That guide does not depend on finding more demand. The CEO says demand is much greater than 70%, and that supply is what holds the guide there. The CFO expects supply to stay a bottleneck at least through the end of fiscal 2028 and calls memory pricing extreme.
Some of that demand also leans on NVIDIA’s own balance sheet. NVIDIA has invested nearly $50 billion in frontier AI labs. Management concedes some will call this circular financing and says the compute can be redeployed to other customers.
Analysts also disagree on what that growth earns. The 33 estimates of fiscal 2028 earnings run from $12.62 to $17.49 a share, a wide band for the year the forward multiple is priced on.
And NVIDIA’s Gross Margin Is Guided Lower As Memory Costs Climb
Consensus has earnings and revenue growing at a similar pace between fiscal 2027 and fiscal 2028, so analysts assume profit margins hold roughly steady. Gross margin is guided lower. Memory prices have risen more than management expected, and the guide has that margin settling in the 72% to 73% range in fiscal 2028, helped by price increases NVIDIA has already executed.
For the forecast to hold, that lost gross margin has to be made up further down the income statement. That is the second condition behind the fiscal 2028 multiple, alongside supply.
So Is NVIDIA Cheap, Or Only If The Chips Get Built?
Only if the chips get built, which means the supply arrives and the margins hold. The trailing multiple cannot tell you either. How much would you stake on a year that has not started?
Checking that for every stock you own is a job most people have no time for. Since its inception, our rule-based Trefis High Quality Portfolio has outperformed its benchmark, a blend of three major indices.

What Does NVIDIA Offer That Texas Instruments Does Not?

trefis.com · 2026-09-25

the stored text (3,519 characters)
What Does NVIDIA Offer That Texas Instruments Does Not?
Texas Instruments (TXN) and NVIDIA both sell chips for the data centers now being built. Data centers bring in nearly all of NVIDIA’s revenue, while TI sells to industrial and automotive customers as well as data centers. You pay 38.2 times the yearly profit for TI, yet only 28.1 times for NVIDIA. Their latest earnings calls show the two businesses parting ways on supply.
NVIDIA’s data center revenue was $89 billion in fiscal Q2 2027, out of $96 billion in total. Management guided fiscal Q3 revenue to $108 billion, plus or minus 2%. That midpoint is about 12% above the Q2 total. Management expects supply to remain a bottleneck at least through the end of fiscal 2028.
TI guided Q3 2026 revenue to $5.65 billion to $6.15 billion, against $5.5 billion in Q2. TI’s data center sales doubled from a year earlier in Q2. Management said most of the Q3 growth will come from selling more units, not from higher prices. TI also has clean room space it can equip if demand keeps rising.
The two outlooks are different kinds of news. Demand for NVIDIA’s chips is larger than NVIDIA’s supply, so supply sets the pace. TI has spare room to make more, so demand sets TI’s pace.
Each company pays for its position. Memory prices rose more than NVIDIA expected, and NVIDIA reset its margin expectations. Gross margin is the share of sales left after production costs. NVIDIA guided it to 74% for fiscal Q3, down from 75% in Q2. NVIDIA expects gross margin to bottom at 71% to 72% in fiscal Q4.
NVIDIA has also invested nearly $50 billion in AI labs. Management expects AI labs backed by NVIDIA’s balance sheet to make up roughly a quarter of NVIDIA’s business in fiscal 2028. That part of NVIDIA’s business would rest on customers NVIDIA helps fund.
TI’s cost is its factories. TI spent $3.3 billion on capital projects in the year to Q2 2026. TI’s $8.7 billion of cash from operations covered that more than twice over. TI’s free cash flow over the same period included $1.6 billion of CHIPS Act incentives. The yearly numbers on growth, margin, and debt show how far apart the two already are.
This comparison scores six standard measures of price, growth, profit and debt , and NVIDIA leads Texas Instruments on all six. NVIDIA’s revenue grew 83% over the past year, against 16.7% for TI. NVIDIA kept 65.2 cents of each sales dollar as operating profit, against 38.1 cents at TI.
Debt equals 0.7% of NVIDIA’s market value, against 6.1% for TI. NVIDIA carries almost no debt against its value.
TI wins on one measure outside those six: price to sales. You pay 11.9 times the yearly revenue for TI, against 17.9 times for NVIDIA. The six scored measures judge price on profit, and on profit NVIDIA is cheaper.
TI also has a long dividend record. In September, TI raised the quarterly dividend to $1.52 a share, from $1.42. That was TI’s 23rd straight year of dividend increases.
What Would Count As A Stumble For NVIDIA?
NVIDIA’s lead is wide enough that it would take a real stumble to shrink it. Revenue below NVIDIA’s own guided range for fiscal Q3 would be the first sign of one. NVIDIA holds its call on that quarter on November 17. A result inside or above the range would leave NVIDIA’s lead in place.
NVIDIA leads on growth, margins, debt, and price on profit. TI’s strengths are a lower price on sales, spare capacity, and a long dividend record. Revenue below NVIDIA’s guided range in November is the first result that would test that lead.

Nvidia Would Have to Add Almost a Whole Broadcom to Reach $7 Trillion

fool.com · 2026-09-25

the stored text (4,290 characters)
Nvidia ( NVDA +2.55% ) , the world's most valuable company, is worth around $5.45 trillion as of this writing. The next big round number is $7 trillion. And the gap between them is larger than it sounds: roughly $1.55 trillion, or nearly the full market value of Broadcom ( AVGO +2.83% ) , the $1.69 trillion chip and infrastructure software business.
What would have to be true of Nvidia's revenue, its margins, and its price-to-earnings multiple for the company to add almost a whole Broadcom to its value? And how long could it take?
After all, fewer than 10 other companies in the world are worth more than the gap itself. At Nvidia's size, even a modest-sounding percentage gain today matches an entire giant company.
Step back, though, and the climb looks smaller than the dollars suggest. From around $225 as I write this, a $7 trillion market cap comes out to around $290 a share -- a rise of around 28%.
For context, shares have climbed 37% from their 52-week low, and they now trade within around 5% of their 52-week high. I'd say nothing about a 28% move is unusual for this stock.
Nvidia's latest results make the growth requirement seem nearly routine. Revenue in the fiscal 2027 second quarter (the period ended July 26, 2026) came to $96.2 billion, up 106% over a year earlier. This pace was even faster than the fiscal first quarter's 85% year-over-year growth. The data center business made up $89 billion of the total. And net income , at $59.7 billion, rose 126% year over year.
"Now, compute is revenue," said CEO Jensen Huang in the company's second-quarter earnings release.
The forecasts go far past the quarter. Guidance for the fiscal third quarter is $108 billion in revenue , around 12% above fiscal Q2. And on the company's late-August earnings call, CFO Colette Kress said Nvidia expects revenue to rise roughly 70% in fiscal 2028, the year ending in January 2028. She called it "a supply-constrained outlook." Put another way, the limit is manufacturing capacity, not customer demand.
Analysts' consensus already has fiscal 2027 revenue coming in around $412 billion. Grow that by roughly 70%, and fiscal 2028 lands somewhere around $700 billion.
Margins might not change the picture much, either. On the call, Kress said gross margins should bottom between 71% and 72% in the fiscal fourth quarter and then settle at 72% to 73% in fiscal 2028 as price hikes kick in. That'd be a drop from the fiscal second quarter's 75%, though a small one.
When might it happen?
Nvidia's stock costs around 14 times forward earnings today, using analysts' fiscal 2028 estimates. At $290 per share, that forward price-to-earnings multiple would sit closer to 18 -- around what Broadcom gets today on the same basis.
Fiscal 2028 ends in January 2028, roughly 16 months from now. If Nvidia delivers the year it has forecast, and the market prices those earnings the way it prices Broadcom's today, $7 trillion arrives somewhere on that timeline.
The stock could also get there without any increase in its price-to-earnings multiple. Hold that multiple of expected earnings flat, and shares hit $290 when expectations for fiscal 2029 are around 28% over fiscal 2028's. Next to this fiscal year's expected 90% revenue growth and next year's forecast 70%, that would arguably be a low bar. But nothing about fiscal 2029 is guided yet. And estimates that far out can change a lot.
Of course, the valuation multiple is also the half Nvidia doesn't control. Shares have risen roughly 21% in 2026 as of this writing, a year when quarterly profit more than doubled. That mismatch is the price-to-earnings multiple shrinking, quarter by quarter.
If the shrinking continues at anything like this year's pace, no forecast growth rate could take the stock to $7 trillion on this timeline.
In the end, what separates this milestone from past ones is that the growth to support it is already forecast. Reaching $7 trillion doesn't need a reacceleration or a richer valuation than Broadcom gets today.
I think the earnings will show up more or less as forecast. What the market chooses to pay for them is another question.

Meet the Magnificent Semiconductor Stock Crushing Nvidia in 2026

fool.com · 2026-09-25

the stored text (2,637 characters)
Nvidia ( NVDA +2.55% ) has been one of the market's most popular stocks over the past few years. With a market capitalization of over $5.45 trillion, it's the world's most valuable public company, aided by its 21% gains so far this year (as of Sept. 23).
Despite Nvidia's impressive returns this year, it doesn't compare to Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing ( TSM +2.34% ) (TSMC), which is up over 46%. The semiconductor giant has a lucrative relationship with Nvidia, and right now, its business is capitalizing more than ever.
TSMC is the world's largest semiconductor foundry. Its foundry business model means that instead of making chips for general sale, it makes chips for companies' specific products.
For example, Nvidia's GPUs have become critical to the current AI boom (which is largely why the company has become so valuable); however, Nvidia doesn't actually make them. It designs them, and TSMC manufactures and brings them to life.
Manufacturing chips is hard and expensive, and it requires a lot of expertise. That's why companies instead rely on TSMC -- which has been doing it since 1987. TSMC is the go-to manufacturer for Nvidia, Apple , Microsoft , Alphabet , Amazon , and almost every major tech company .
High demand comes with high pricing power
TSMC's business has thrived for a while, but the current AI arms race has done wonders for it. As companies spend billions to build data centers and other AI infrastructure, demand for TSMC's manufacturing capabilities has grown. This has given TSMC unmatched pricing power and sent profits soaring.
In the second quarter, TSMC's revenue rose 33.7% year over year to $40.2 billion, but more impressive is how much its margins have expanded. Over the past year, its gross margin rose from 58.6% to 67.7%, operating margin from 49.6% to 60.3%, and net profit margin from 42.7% to 55.6%.
TSMC is making more revenue and profit than ever, and that's likely to continue for the next couple of years as the AI infrastructure build-out continues. It expects Q3 revenue between $44.6 billion and $45.8 billion, which would be 34.7% to 38.4% higher than its $33.1 billion in revenue in Q3 2025.
Trading at 26.5 times its projected earnings over the next 12 months, TSMC's stock isn't "cheap." However, it's worth the premium for a company that's by far the most dominant in its industry and will likely remain so because of the high barriers to entry.
TSMC is one of my largest holdings, and I plan to continue adding to my stake. Its importance to the tech world, even outside of AI, almost ensures continued long-term success.

3 Reasons Why Nvidia Fits Warren Buffett's Investment Style

fool.com · 2026-09-27

the stored text (3,950 characters)
While Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway have never owned Nvidia ( NVDA +2.55% ) , the chipmaker actually fits the investment profile of the stocks he loves to own. The semiconductor stock has a wide moat with a durable, compounding business model that is trading at an attractive valuation.
Let's dig deeper into why Nvidia should be considered a Buffett-style investment .
Nvidia didn't stumble into becoming the king of artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure by accident. It was a carefully orchestrated plan that took many years to pay off, but it resulted in a wide moat in the space.
It all started with its CUDA software platform , which the company created to let developers program its powerful graphics processing units (GPUs) for tasks outside of their original purpose of speeding up graphics rendering in video games. Nvidia then took CUDA and planted it into universities and research labs that were doing early work on AI. That resulted in an entire generation of AI engineers trained on its software platform and most foundational AI code written using its software and optimized for its chips.
Those moves formed the wide moat that Nvidia sees in AI infrastructure today, particularly with regard to large language model (LLM) training.
However, the company did not stop there, designing a whole AI infrastructure ecosystem around CUDA and its GPUs. It developed its proprietary NVLink interconnect technology to help its chips act as a single unit, further widening its moat, and in 2020 it acquired next-gen networking company Mellanox, whose technology was arguably ahead of its time. Earlier this year, Nvidia "acquired" Groq to bring its language processing unit (LPU) technology into the CUDA ecosystem, putting Nvidia at the forefront of the fast-growing inference market.
Nvidia has also made other smart moves to make sure its moat remains intact. It locked in $500 billion worth of supply constrained high-bandwidth memory (HBM), which is needed to optimize AI chips, and it also acquired important open-source AI systems in SchedMD and HuggingFace to move into the critical software layers used to orchestrate and distribute AI.
In the span of two decades, Nvidia has gone from a video game chipmaker to a complete end-to-end infrastructure player with a wide and perhaps growing moat.
Warren Buffett championed Berkshire's investment in Alphabet and gave the go-ahead to invest an additional $10 billion in a private placement to help the company build out the AI infrastructure for its cloud computing unit. As such, he clearly believes in cloud computing economics.
When using Nvidia chips, cloud providers have typically been getting paybacks within two to three years, while the chips tend to have economic life spans of five to six years. That gives Nvidia a visible replacement cycle that is pretty similar to Buffett's top holding, Apple , with its iPhones and computers.
Meanwhile, the great thing about AI versus past huge tech trends, like the internet and smartphones, is that its demand can essentially be limitless if it continues to provide a strong economic payback, not bound by human consumption. Combined with a well-defined replacement cycle, Nvidia should have a strong compounding business even when the AI frenzy starts to calm down.
Even with great businesses, Buffett isn't willing to pay any price. He wants to buy stocks when they are trading below their intrinsic value, and Nvidia can certainly make that argument.
The stock trades at a forward P/E of 24 times fiscal 2027 (ending January 2027) and just above 14 times the fiscal 2028 consensus. This is for a company with about 75% gross margins that grew its revenue by more than 100% last quarter and projected that it would increase its revenue by around 70% next fiscal year.
While revenue growth will eventually slow, Nvidia is a stock worth a lot more than it is currently valued.

now, and at least through the end of fiscal 2028
NVIDIA reports fiscal Q3 2027 results
NVIDIA’s Fiscal Q3 Report Lands On November 17
NVIDIA reports its fiscal Q3 2027 results after the market closes on November 17, 2026. Management has guided revenue to $108 billion , plus or minus 2%.

NVIDIA cannot build enough for now. Management expects supply to stay a bottleneck at least through the end of fiscal 2028. On the fiscal Q2 2027 call, management said its entire supply chain is stretched.

If a loss of that size would push you to sell, your position is too large for these dates. Size it so a repeat of that fall is a loss you could sit through. NVIDIA needs its margin to stop where management said it would, and revenue to land near its guide.

Nvidia's record stock buyback funded by cash from the data center boom

marketplace.org · 2026-09-28

the stored text (2,988 characters)
Nvidia's record stock buyback funded by cash from the data center boom
Companies that used to be flush with cash are now borrowing to buy more from the chipmaker.
“Companies are filling the dump trucks with money and sending it to Nvidia to buy their chips. Nvidia is getting dump trucks of money every day,” said Erik Gordon, a business professor at the University of Michigan.
The chipmaker Nvidia announced that it’s going to buy back even more of its own stock over the next couple years — a record $150 billion more on top of the $85 billion already promised. It’s the biggest buyback in history, surpassing Apple’s previous record.
Buying back stock is one of a few ways a company can return cash to shareholders, according to Nick Guest, an assistant professor of accounting at Cornell. Another way is that a company could pay a dividend for every share.
“They tend to be regular, and the market tends to build up an expectation for them, so that if you pay a dividend this quarter, you're going to pay at least that much in future quarters,” Guest said.
But buying shares from shareholders is more flexible, Guest explained. So stock buybacks have become the favored way for companies with excess profits to share them.
“They said, ‘Hey, we don't have that many investment opportunities. We can't use all this cash right now, so we're going to give some of it back to you, investors, and then you can decide what to do with it,’” Guest said.
So far, Apple has been the leader in stock buybacks. But Alphabet, Meta, and Oracle also spent heavily to repurchase their own shares — at least, until this year.
“We're seeing something I never thought we would see, which is tech companies, which formerly were just bringing in the cash, now are spending huge amounts of cash on AI,” said Erik Gordon, a business professor at the University of Michigan.
The companies spending big on data centers have seen the buildout eat into their free cash flow. Some are selling new stock, and most are borrowing to fund construction.
“Companies are filling the dump trucks with money and sending it to Nvidia to buy their chips. Nvidia is getting dump trucks of money every day,” said Gordon. “That's why it can do stock buybacks.”
Of course, Nvidia doesn’t have to buy back stock with its excess profits. Jacob Bourne, a tech analyst at eMarketer, wondered if the company could spend the money on something else.
“Why is this the best bet for Nvidia right now, as opposed to acquiring more AI startups, or investing more in chip development?” Bourne asked.
But Nvidia is doing those things, too, according to Daniel Newman, an analyst with Futurum.
“When you have a balance sheet that looks like Nvidia, you have the optionality to return to shareholders, and you have the optionality to invest in the ecosystem,” Newman said.
Nvidia has committed hundreds of billions to the chip supply chain, as well as invested in AI companies that then turn around and spend their money on more Nvidia chips.

Will Nvidia be allowed to sell more chips to China? This former Trump economic adviser has a hot take.

finance.yahoo.com · 2026-09-23

the stored text (1,833 characters)
Will Nvidia be allowed to sell more chips to China? This former Trump economic adviser has a hot take.
When Nvidia ( NVDA ) CEO Jensen Huang arrives at the state dinner on Thursday between President Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping, you better believe he'll be hoping to get a sense of when — and to what extent — his business may resume in China.
Both sides coming to a deal on this front won't be easy, given the politics still at play.
"My personal opinion is that we need to have as much competitive advantage here in the United States as we can, because we've got competitive disadvantages against China," said Gary Cohn, IBM ( IBM ) vice chair and former economic adviser to President Trump in his first term, on Yahoo Finance's Sozzi Unleashed . "You know, they don't have environmental issues. They don't have a cost of capital. They can build data centers wherever they want. The citizens can't stand up and say not in my backyard."
Cohn added, "To the extent that the Chinese have all these other significant advantages, the one advantage we have is we've got the chips, and we've got the chip manufacturing here. I'd like to keep that advantage as long as we can. "
The Trump administration has allowed Nvidia to ship its H200 AI chips to approved customers in China. The problem for Nvidia: The H200 is already an older-generation product, trailing Nvidia's current Blackwell chips in terms of performance.
In other words, Nvidia is likely leaving billions in sales on the table by being unable to get Blackwell chips into China to feed the country's AI startup scene.
Chinese authorities, however, have so far restricted shipments of Nvidia's H200 chips.
While Nvidia hasn't factored any sales from China into its outlook for the year, the stock is still up 21% year to date, powered by the AI boom here in the US.

China's AI chip blitz arms Xi with a message for Trump: 'You can't choke us off'

cnbc.com · 2026-09-23

the stored text (6,223 characters)
A run of new chip and AI models from national champions gives Chinese President Xi Jinping a confidence boost, as the U.S. summit gets underway.
The blitz may be part of an effort to show Chinese firms are "catching up", experts said, though they remain dependent on the U.S. for access to advanced chips.
The timing of the tech giants' flagship releases has revived debate over whether China's AI industry has hit an inflection point.
SHANGHAI, CHINA - JULY 17: Chinese President Xi Jinping during the opening ceremony of the High-Level Meeting on Global Governance for the World AI Conference in Shanghai, Friday, July 17, 2026.
Chinese President Xi Jinping has kicked off his first U.S. state visit in more than a decade with a confidence boost — a run of chip and model launches that could give Washington pause before tightening tech export controls further.
Recent releases of new chip and AI models from national champions, including Huawei Technologies and Alibaba Group , have sharpened Beijing's case that a key lever used by Washington against Chinese AI has limits: its technology restrictions can slow China's ambitions but not stop them.
"For Xi, the leverage is not 'we have caught up with you' — it's 'you can't simply technologically choke us off,'" said Lizzi Lee, a fellow at the Asia Society Policy Institute.
Washington has for years restricted China's access to Nvidia's most advanced AI chips and the equipment to make them, betting that starving computing power would keep Chinese AI a generation behind. Silicon Valley executives have also accused Chinese AI companies of using "distillation" — training models on the output of more advanced U.S. systems, a claim Beijing rejects.
Against that backdrop, Huawei brought the release of its next AI chip forward by three quarters . Alibaba unveiled what it called the country's most powerful AI processor . DeepSeek, the startup whose low-cost model rattled global markets last year, reportedly lined up the largest known cluster of Huawei chips built to date.
"Xi's confidence level in terms of how great China is in terms of technology and innovation is at a historic high," said George Chen, partner and chair of digital practice at Washington-based policy consultancy The Asia Group. "You need to have cards when you sit at a table, and Xi has many cards," Chen said, calling the timing a "deliberate" confidence boost ahead of the leaders' summit.
Xi's visit to the White House — his first in 11 years — follows Trump's state visit to Beijing in May. The timing of the tech giants' flagship releases, days ahead of the summit, has revived debate over whether China's AI industry has hit an inflection point.
Chris Miller, a Tufts University professor and author of "Chip War", pointed out that China has a history of announcing new projects around meetings with senior policymakers as part of an effort to show Chinese firms are "catching up," he said.
But U.S. models remain ahead, he said, on technical benchmarks and profitability. American firms like Anthropic and OpenAI make 50 to 100 times the revenue of comparable Chinese firms, he said.
At its annual Huawei Connect conference last week, Huawei said its Ascent 960DT will ship in the first quarter of 2027 , three quarters ahead of its original schedule. At the Apsara Conference in Hangzhou on Tuesday, Alibaba's chip unit unveiled the Zhenwu V900 chip , which triples the performance of its predecessor and will go on sale in early 2027.
SHANGHAI, CHINA - JULY 17 2026: A view of the AI chip Zhenwu M890, built on a self-developed parallel computing architecture by Alibaba's T-Head, at the World AI Conference in Shanghai, China, Friday, July 17, 2026.
"There does seem to be genuine acceleration in its production timeline," Sigrid Wang, analyst at China-focused Hutong Research, said of Huawei's chip. The clustering "gives Xi more examples to point to when arguing that U.S. restrictions can slow China's technological development but have not prevented Chinese companies from finding alternative paths forward," Wang said.
Chinese firms have sought to make up for weaker individual chips by linking thousands of them into large clusters over faster connections.
But the approach has limits. Huawei's latest SuperPoD computing architecture links far fewer processors than originally planned, and each chip still delivers roughly half the compute of Nvidia's. Chairman Eric Xu acknowledged that Huawei may not be able to make enough chips to meet domestic demand, and that Chinese AI developers may not be advanced enough to experience rogue safety risks.
"Chinese chip companies haven't made any groundbreaking progress in the past few years," said Xiaomeng Lu, a director at Eurasia Group. "The fundamental competitive landscape hasn't changed."
Chinese firms remain substantially short of the chips needed to train and deploy advanced models, and rely heavily on smuggling chips or gaining access to data centers outside China, Miller said. "China is still highly dependent on the U.S. for advanced chips."
As Trump hosts Xi for a multi-day state visit, neither side appears ready to put chip export controls on the table.
Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer met Vice Premier He Lifeng in New York over the weekend and set up a formal AI channel for incident warnings. But Greer said explicitly that export controls on advanced chips and chipmaking equipment were not on the agenda.
"It's striking to see the chip export control issue not come up at these summits given how often Xi brought them up with Biden," Chan said. The Chinese president has also not invited a delegation of business executives and tech leaders to the summit, according to media reports .
Beijing's immediate priorities may lie elsewhere. Lyle Morris, a senior fellow at the Asia Society Policy Institute, said Xi is likely to press Trump to align closer on the Taiwan issue, and in return, the Chinese leader may offer help on Iran, including restoring free movement of shipping in the Strait of Hormuz.
"The recalibration of U.S.-China tech relations takes a lot of substantial work," Lee said. "Not a summit deliverable, I am afraid — at least not this time."

According to him, Trump values power and business success. ABC News Australia’s sources believe that the alignment of the president’s and Nvidia chief’s positions could have far-reaching effects on AI development.
Huang is calling on Washington to ease restrictions on sales of advanced AI chips to China. He argues that removing Nvidia from the Chinese market only accelerates Beijing’s shift to domestically produced chips. In his view, US companies should compete more actively in global markets and speed up development rather than slow down because of stricter safety rules.
More current news is available on the UA.News Telegram channel Telegram .
Nvidia’s most powerful chips are currently prohibited from export to China due to Washington’s restrictions. At the same time, last year Trump allowed Nvidia to sell previous-generation H200 chips to approved Chinese buyers with a 25% tariff.

Nvidia is valued at about $5.4 trillion, making it the world’s most valuable company amid growing demand for AI equipment and data centers. The company does not manufacture chips itself: it designs them and outsources production to Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company and other partners.
